#2749. Stuck: How a frozen housing market is hitting buyers, sellers and the economy

Autumn is usually a busy time in the housing market, but not this year. A sharp spike in mortgage rates in September has pushed the already chilly real estate sector into a deep freeze.

Buyers and sellers are moving to the sidelines, leaving those in the real estate business without much business.

The average rate on the popular 30-year fixed mortgage hovered around 6.60% in July, according to Mortgage News Daily. By the start of October, it was at 7.61%, a full percentage point higher.

"That shock, that rise of 100 basis points, is one of the fastest three-month increases we've seen in decades," said Zillow CEO Jeremy Wacksman on CNBC.

While the jump in rates exacerbated the problem, the slowdown in the housing market has been building for years. Rising home prices have pushed the dream of homeownership out of reach for a growing number of Americans. The number of active buyers nationally has been declining for six straight months and in October hit its lowest level since the aftermath of the financial crisis, according to Redfin.

The supply of homes for sale is also low. Builders are now faced with higher costs for land, labor, materials and energy. They simply can't afford to build the cheaper homes that are currently in demand. Single-family building permits, an indicator of future construction, were down 13% in October year over year, according to the U.S. Census. And builder sentiment in November dropped to the lowest level since 2012.

This slowdown in the entire housing ecosystem is impacting every facet of Americans' lives and livelihoods.

"Where you live determines so much. It determines where your kids go to school. It determines the jobs that you have access to, right? It determines whether or not you're able to be close to your family," said Chen Zhao, chief economist at Redfin. "What we've seen with the housing market these past four years, with this historically low level of turnover, is that a lot of people really are stuck in their home."

Closed sales of existing homes fell to a 16-year low in October, according to the National Association of Realtors. Pending sales, an indicator of future closings, hit a record low on the 25-year-old index from the Realtors.

While the pace of home price increases has slowed recently, prices are up well over 50% from pre-pandemic levels. Part of that is because homebuilding has slowed to a crawl.

It all has Ryan Deighan, who wants to buy a home, wondering if he ever will.

"I genuinely don't know, and that's the scary part," he said.

Deighan and his wife have been renting in Boston for six years. He is a chiropractor; she works in private equity. Even with a combined salary of more than $200,000 a year, he says they're stuck.

"Making what I am and what my wife makes, it just feels like we're on paper doing well, but it feels like we are still kind of fighting to put down certain amounts toward a house," Deighan said.

Being priced out of homeownership, he said, is changing what he called his "checklist" of how he once pictured his future.

"You graduate college, you find someone, you get married, you have the home with the white picket fence and the dog, and then the kids," he said. "That just looks a little different for us."

The numbers don't just affect would-be buyers. They are hitting sellers, too.

Robin Welch has been living in her Cleveland-area home for more than 20 years. Her children are now grown and moved out. She would like to downsize to a condominium, but the math doesn't make sense — in part, because her current interest rate is much lower than any new rate she could get. This has been dubbed the mortgage "lock-in effect."

"It actually costs more money to buy less of a home," said Welch. "I would have to pay into an HOA, which would be another monthly expense on top of increased property taxes and increased interest rate."

Welch has also done upgrades to her home over the years and doesn't want to have to start doing that on another home.

CNBC's Property Play with Diana Olick covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, delivered weekly to your inbox.

Welch is part of why the housing turnover rate, which is how often homes get new owners, has now dropped to just 3.8% a year, well below the 5% rate in the decade following the financial crisis, according to Cotality.

In addition, the national mobility rate, which is how often people move from region to region, has been falling for two years. It now sits at 8.3%, down from 11.1% a decade ago, according to Harvard's Joint Center for Housing Studies tabulations of U.S. Census Bureau data. Mobility hit a high of nearly 18% in the late 1980s.

"It's largely because of the interest lock-in effect, but also because of home prices just being so high," said Riordan Frost, senior research analyst at Harvard's JCHS. "Those things combined have made it hard both for existing homeowners to move and for people to get into homeownership."

Americans have nearly $35 trillion of their wealth locked up in housing, a record high, according to Cotality. But the slowdown in the housing market, Frost said, impacts the accumulation, handover and use of that wealth. It also affects the labor market.

"Any kind of potential job would have to really outweigh … the price of taking that mortgage with a higher rate, or the price of moving, or the price of getting into a higher rent unit," Frost said.

The wealth gap between homeowners and renters now is nothing short of massive. The most recent survey of consumer finances last year by the Federal Reserve showed that the median homeowner net wealth is about 43 times the median renter net wealth.

"It has kind of knock-on effects for the rest of the economy. It has effects on the labor market and how good of a match we can get between employees and employers. It has an effect on education. It has an effect on just GDP growth," said Zhao.

When homes aren't selling and people aren't moving, those who work in that business are stuck as well. The entire housing ecosystem is churning much more slowly, and the sudden jump in mortgage rates is causing deals to fall apart.

"Right after Labor Day, we went live with a bunch of listings. They all went under contract over the asking price," said Dana Bull, a real estate agent in the Boston suburbs. But then rates spiked.

"It was a shock to the system," Bull said. "I think all of them came back on the market. We fell out of contract." Asked if she is seeing colleagues leaving the market, she answered: "People are thinking about it. It's tough."

The economy has lost close to 2,000 jobs in real estate and leasing over the past year and 4,400 jobs in residential construction, according to the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics.

Jobs in the mortgage industry have been falling since June and are down 38% since their last peak in the second quarter of 2021, according to the Mortgage Bankers Association. That was when mortgage rates were less than half what they are today.

Shant Banosian, president of Rate and the top mortgage originator in the U.S. by dollar volume, said his business is down about 30% this past year.

"Whenever change happens quickly, there's a slowdown," said Banosian, who has been in the business for 23 years. "It requires more of us to go out and advise, educate and guide. So, what I experienced was I just needed to spend more time on marketing, branding, relationship building. The conversations are longer because people want more information to try to navigate this market."

Bull agrees. She said her job has gotten harder as well.

"We've done double the work this year, having homes go under contract, fall out of contract, putting them back under agreement, but definitely more negotiating," she said. "That's sort of my plea to both buyers and sellers: We need to get back to a space where we're comfortable negotiating."

#2746. Draymond Green jabs at critics saying he needs to score more: 'That would never be my mindset'

Draymond Green rarely, if ever, refuses an opportunity to jab back at his critics.

Following the Golden State Warriors' 88-87 win over the Boston Celtics on Sunday, the veteran forward had plenty to say to those who believe he's not carrying enough of the scoring burden with Stephen Curry sidelined due to an abdominal tear.

Green scored 9 points in the Warriors' win, his highest total of the season thus far. But scoring more has never been his role and he doesn't intend to change that, even with the team's top scorer out.

"I'm never going to, in Year 15, be like 'Oh, man, Steph's out — it's my chance,'" Green told reporters, via The Athletic's Jason Jones. "When I was in Year 6, it was my chance, and I would do what I do.

"But Year 15? That would never be my mindset," he added. "As someone who can do a multitude of things on the basketball court, I would never deduce myself down to you gotta go score."

All nine of Green's points on Sunday came in the first quarter when he hit three 3-pointers. Green said after the game that Warriors coach Steve Kerr told him to look to shoot from the corner. That helped Golden State run out to a 27-22 lead after the opening 12 minutes of play.

The Celtics went ahead with a third-quarter surge, leading by 11 at one point. But the Warriors tightened up on defense, allowing them to chip away at the second-half deficit. Golden State finally took an 88-86 lead with 2:38 remaining in regulation on a 3-pointer from Brandin Podziemski.

On Boston's final possession, Green prevented Jayson Tatum from driving to the basket and forced him to take a fadeaway jumper. But the 16-footer missed and the Warriors got their ninth win of the season.

Tatum was held to 4 points in the second half with Green defending him. That's exactly what Golden State needs Green to do, according to Kerr. Green offered to come off the bench if it would help the team, but Kerr said his defense is too valuable throughout the game.

"I told him I had no desire to bring him on the bench because we've got to dig this thing out with defense," Kerr said, via The Athletic. "And he remains one of the great defenders in the league. He showed that tonight."

Green is averaging 3.6 points in 14 games this season. If that continues, it would be his lowest scoring average since his rookie campaign in 2012-13. He's also grabbing 4.8 rebounds and dishing out 6.3 assists while shooting 33% on 21 3-point attempts.

#2745. Tokenized stocks face pushback as NYSE, Nasdaq pivot for a new market

OKXICE, a trading platform for tokenized securities jointly operated by Intercontinental Exchange and crypto exchange OKX, is set to debut Monday despite objections from 90% of the companies whose stocks it initially proposed to list.

Of the 62 companies whose shares OKXICE first sought to trade, 56 objected to having their shares tokenized and traded on the platform, according to a review of public notices posted between Oct. 4 and Nov. 5. By last week, even more companies had objected, including those not on the original roster.

The high objection rate may reflect companies' lack of readiness to have blockchain-based representations of their shares traded on a new third-party platform like OKXICE, rather than broad resistance to tokenization.

People involved in talks with some of the companies say a 30-day objection window left them little time to react and decide if they were ready to participate.

"The shift to a 24/7 global marketplace is revolutionary and transformational for our market structure," Andrew Cuomo, co-chair of OKXICE, told CNBC. "The majority of companies aren't familiar with [tokenization] and haven't had a chance to understand it and the consequences."

"Logic dictates that many companies were going to be cautious before they understood what the system would do," added Cuomo, who served 10 years as governor of New York until 2021. "What you're seeing now is the more the companies learn about 24/7 global access, the more receptive they are."

At launch, tokenized shares of Coinbase, Robinhood, Strategy, Circle, Securitize and BitGo will be available to trade on the platform, OKXICE disclosed in a public notice. OKXICE has confirmed 113 assets, including exchange-traded funds and products, going live on a rolling basis. Since its initial pre-launch announcement on Nov. 11, OKXICE said it's seen further interest from different issuers and Fidelity Investments, Direxion, eToro, Strive and Vida Global are now participating.

The 30-day opt-out window is a requirement of the SEC's so-called Innovation Exemption, which took effect in September and temporarily permits the trading of certain tokenized stocks. Under the exemption, trading platforms must notify the underlying issuers and give them an opportunity to object. Any issuer who objects blocks its shares from being tokenized.

"We've gotten a lot of feedback that the reason why you've seen this high number of rejections of tokenizing equity is because it was a 30-day window," Seth Ginns, chief investment officer at Franklin Templeton's crypto arm, told CNBC at the Digital Assets Forum in New York earlier this month. Many issuers were open to exploring the idea but were hesitant to move forward amid competing priorities, he said.

"[They're] saying it's earnings season, there was the midterms, there's a lot going on during this 30-day window. Give us some time, we want to learn about it, we're not closed to the idea we're just closed to rushing into it right now," Ginns added.

Ran Grushkowsky, CEO of MassPay, a global payout platform, said several of his company's clients were among the initial targets and shared that their concern was less about the concept of tokenization and more about how 24/7 trading could affect stock value and investor relations strategies, particularly around the timing of corporate announcements.

"It's more [about] what's going to be the effect, how is it going to actually drive the value of the stock," he said. "A lot of the strategy that happens around investors relations has to do with scheduling and when you announce certain things and so how do you do it when your stock is being traded 24/7?"

"The executives were actually quite excited about this new innovation and potentially getting more interest into the stock. It was really from the [investor relations] side that they received push back," and heard "let's hold off," Grushkowsky added.

The lack of awareness may even extend to executives overseeing digital asset initiatives, suggesting that some rejections stem from internal communication gaps and unfamiliarity with the process, according to Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo, whose company is one of the first being listed on OKX. Securitize is also the tokenization partner for OKX.

"We've talked to some of the issuers that have opted out [where] the head of digital assets doesn't even know who in the company rejected them," he said. "This is a consequence of, first, the novelty of the process and of these venues, and second, the lack of understanding of what they're being notified to do."

Despite the initial pushback, Domingo expects more issuers to participate as companies become more familiar with the model, noting that some regulated trading venues can operate without issuer consent.

Company objections raise a separate question about which of the different tokenization models will prove most attractive. The main alternative to the OKXICE model is an issuer-led approach that integrates blockchain-based shares into existing regulated market infrastructures, with issuers retaining greater control over the process.

Bullish, a digital asset platform, is pursuing an example of this model through its partnership with Equiniti, the transfer-agent business it's acquiring for $4 billion.

Nasdaq's planned launch of an issuer-centric tokenized equities model in partnership with Kraken parent Payward in the second quarter of next year could offer a test of that framework and whether companies are more willing to embrace tokenization under a different structure.

Nasdaq and the New York Stock Exchange, owned by Intercontinental, and others are responding to a structural shift in how securities are issued, traded and settled. Tokenization has become attractive to institutions and investors because it enables round the clock stock trading, shortened settlement times, fractional ownership and eases the use of securities as collateral.

For the exchanges, it presents a way to keep control of trading and market infrastructure as crypto-native, blockchain-based venues seek to bring traditional financial assets on to blockchain. It's a long way off before it becomes a meaningful revenue driver, however, according to Clear Street analyst Owen Lau.

"It increases the [total addressable market], that's the number one thing," Lau said. "In the near term, there may not be enough of an incremental revenue opportunity, but the trend is there. The trend is to tokenize equities, the trend is to facilitate 24/7 trading."

#2743. Nolan Traoré set to miss 8-10 weeks, Nets announce

Just as Nolan Traoré was starting to get hot for the Brooklyn Nets, he and they were dealt some unfortunate news.

After he left Friday night's loss to the Orlando Magic early with a right hamstring injury, Brooklyn announced on Monday morning that he has been diagnosed with a right hamstring strain. He will be reevaluated in eight to ten weeks, the team added.

Despite a 3-12 start to the season, Traoré had been one of the Nets' most promising young pieces. Through 14 games and 10 starts, the 2025 first-round pick is averaging 12.9 points, 1.6 rebounds, and 2.7 assists on 51.1% shooting, including a whopping 40.4% from 3-point range. He also had a career-high 23 points vs the 76ers last week.

One of the biggest areas for growth that Traoré has shown is finishing around the rim. Since entering the league, he'd been regarded as one of the fastest players in the sport, but in his rookie campiagn, he struggled to finish near the basket. According to Synergy, he converted just 44.6% of his shots at the rim—ranking dead last among 230 players with at least 130 attempts.

After an offseason where he bulked up and gained muscle, he has improved significantly in that facet of the game, and the Frenchman has finished 56.4% of his shots near the rim, a significant improvement.

It will be interesting to see how Jordi Fernández and the Nets adjust in his absence. One possibility, of course, is that Mikel Brown Jr. will see more action and perhaps even start. However, he remains on a minutes restriction, Another name worth keeping an eye on is Ben Saraf.

Another 2025 first-round pick and member of the Flatbush 5 has spent most of the season in the G League with Long Island. However, that could change with Traoré sidelined.

The 20-year-old, the team's second youngest player after Traore, has certainly made a case for himself. With Long Island, Saraf is averaging 16.4 points, 3.8 rebounds, and 8.6 assists per game. He is inside the top 10 in the G League in assists per game and near the top in total assists..

Nevertheless, Traoré was just hitting his stride, and now he is facing an extended absence. Not only is it unfortunate for the second-year guard, but it will also be a tough blow for Brooklyn in what has already been a difficult start to the season.

#2742. Jaylen Warren, Luther Burden II, and other Week 11 fantasy football bad beats not to worry about

Jaylen Warren, Luther Burden II, and other Week 11 fantasy football bad beats not to worry about appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

One quiet box score in November is enough to make any fantasy football manager hit the panic button, especially with the playoffs rapidly approaching. Yet chasing single-week results without examining underlying usage is one of the fastest ways to sabotage a championship contender. With six teams on bye thinning out lineups across ClutchPoints' Week 11 running back rankings and Week 11 wide receiver Start 'Em, Sit 'Em slate, several trusted starters delivered frustrating duds despite healthy roles. Before making any rash benchings or lopsided trades, take a deep breath on these four Week 11 bad beats.

Managers who plugged Warren into their lineups watched him muster just nine carries for 28 rushing yards and two catches for one receiving yard in Pittsburgh's 19-16 road loss to Philadelphia. Finishing with 29 total yards hurts, but game context softens the blow. Aaron Rodgers attempted 44 passes as Pittsburgh struggled to generate running room against a stout Eagles defensive front, capping every Steelers rusher. More importantly, Warren still led Rico Dowdle in carries and ran 36 routes compared to Dowdle's 16, proving his grip on passing-down snaps remains intact. After ripping off elite production from Weeks 3 through 8, Warren stays firmly in the RB2 mix entering Week 12.

Burden burned fantasy rosters during Chicago's 21-13 home victory over New Orleans, catching two of three targets for 10 yards while adding a three-yard carry. That 13-yard output looks alarming until you inspect the Bears' conservative game plan. Playing with a steady lead throughout the afternoon, Chicago leaned heavily on its ground attack while quarterback Caleb Williams threw only 23 passes, leaving no Bears pass-catcher with more than four targets. Burden still paces Chicago with 51 receptions, 654 receiving yards, and four touchdowns across 10 games. Expect a quick rebound when the Bears visit Detroit in a high-scoring Thanksgiving clash.

Two proven wideouts also posted misleading duds in Week 11. Returning from a two-month hamstring injury in Philadelphia's win over Pittsburgh, DeVonta Smith turned 12 targets into 41 receiving yards with Joe Milton under center. Commanding that level of immediate target volume signals huge upside once Smith shakes off the rust, much like the usage-driven plays highlighted in ClutchPoints' Week 11 tight end rankings and Week 11 quarterback Start 'Em, Sit 'Em breakdown.

Meanwhile, New York Jets star Garrett Wilson caught three of five targets for 39 yards in a 27-24 upset over the Los Angeles Chargers. Wilson generated strong yardage after the catch by breaking tackles on multiple receptions and only missed a touchdown because Geno Smith badly overthrew him near the end zone from the six-yard line. Sitting at 53 catches for 672 yards and three scores on the season, Wilson remains an automatic starter against Miami in Week 12.

#2741. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2740. CrowdStrike to buy Zscaler in $44 billion deal as AI supercharges cyberattacks

CrowdStrike plans to buy Zscaler for $43.9 billion in an all-stock deal that combines two significant security businesses at a time when artificial intelligence is supercharging attacks.

Shares of CrowdStrike dropped 9% on Monday, while Zscaler surged nearly 18%.

The deal is expected to close in the second half of CrowdStrike's fiscal year 2028. Under the agreement, Zscaler shareholders will receive 1.135 CrowdStrike shares for each share.

CrowdStrike's acquisition pushes the Austin, Texas-based cyber vendor into the network security market and comes amid a massive year for cybersecurity deal-making in the wake of high-profile hacks like Hugging Face.

"With Zscaler, we're building cybersecurity's operating system: a single control plane to deploy AI, control AI, and enable any agentic application to run safely," CrowdStrike CEO George Kurtz said in a statement. "We see a path to become the largest AI safety and security company the world has ever seen."

Recent outages affecting Microsoft, the Federal Aviation Administration and JPMorgan Chase have sparked anxiety as businesses accelerate software patching and upgrades to aging systems in an effort to prevent bad actors from exploiting software vulnerabilities with AI.

CrowdStrike has been a major beneficiary of skyrocketing demand for AI security, with shares soaring about 80% this year as of Monday's open. Zscaler shares are up about 5% year to date after Monday's surge. Their combined market value exceeded $265 billion as of Friday's close, behind market leader Palo Alto Networks at $282 billion.

The deal marks CrowdStrike's biggest acquisition to date and is among the largest in the sector as businesses consolidate and ramp up defenses to fend off AI-powered attacks.

Last year, Google announced plans to acquire Wiz for $32 billion. In 2024 Cisco shelled about $28 billion for Splunk. Competitor Palo Alto Networks has also spent billions on several cyber and AI businesses, including startup Applied Compute and a $25 billion deal for CyberArk, which it closed earlier this year.

Analysts view the Zscaler deal as a major AI security opportunity, allowing CrowdStrike to capture more customer security spending and close a significant gap with Palo Alto.

"The addition of ZS to CRWD's platform could bring more breadth in an era of vendor consolidation and, more importantly, network security to position the company more effectively for rising demand for AI Sovereignty," wrote analysts at JPMorgan.

CrowdStrike on Monday also reported better-than-expected fiscal third-quarter results, including earnings of 34 cents per share on $1.58 billion in revenue. That topped the 31 cents per share on $1.53 billion in revenue forecast by LSEG.

Zscaler's fiscal first-quarter earnings, also released on Monday, likewise surpassed estimates.

#2738. Knicks Bulletin: ‘I got out of bed and played the game’

I missed yesterday's game, so I had to watch the replay early today.

Good to know the world healed and the Knicks righted all wrongs, beating Indy again to make it three wins in their last four meetings and 5-2 in the past calendar year.

Here are a few quotes straight outta Sunday.

On preferring disciplined defenders over players who chase steals and blocks:

"I've never been a steal guy. I've never been a block guy. I would prefer a guy that relied more on just going vertical at the rim than going for blocks and I would prefer a position guy defensively more than a steal guy.

"Because to me, this is just my personal opinion, when you talk about steals and you talk about blocks, you might get two or three steals per game, but I promise that you're going to miss four to six a game. Meaning if that pass is going [and] I try to jump the lane, and now I miss it, now they have an advantage of five-on-four behind me because I'm out of position.

"Now everybody is scrambling to make up for my miss. If I go to block his shot and I swing down, three out of the four times or three out of the five times, he's going to the free throw line and/or if I'm going to chase a block because I'm a big, now somebody on the backside, or my [opponent] who is seven feet, is getting an offensive rebound because I'm going to block a shot.

"I'm either going to foul him or, every once in a while, I'll block it, everyone will go 'oooh'. Every once in a while I'll get a steal and go dunk it and everyone will go 'oooh', but it's not worth it for me. I've never been that way.

"So if you do the other things and you're solid as a group out on the floor, you have a better chance of moving up in the rating than if you're running all over the place, gambling for steals and/or blocks."

On the Knicks' defense being ahead of their offense despite gradual shooting improvements:

"I didn't know it was down overall. I don't know. I don't know if the defenses are getting better. I do know we, just our team, we haven't shot the ball as well as we'd like. But every game we're getting a little bit better, everybody's percentages are going up just a little bit every game, and that's exciting. I think eventually for us, right now our defense is way ahead of our offense, but our offense is coming, and hopefully we'll get there sooner than later."

On setting Mohamed Diawara and Pacome Dadiet to compete for minutes throughout the season:

"It's gonna be a competition all year, and it's gonna be back and forth."

On giving Dadiet an opportunity after his strong performances in practice:

"I said the first opportunity I get, I'm gonna try to throw [Dadiet] in the game 'cause he's been playing well in practice and stuff like that. And it's just, in my opinion, his turn … but it's not permanent."

On apologizing to the Knicks for failing to make a defensive substitution before Damian Lillard's game-tying three-pointer:

"I apologized to my team. I wanted to make a defensive substitution to get Tyler [Kolek] off the floor at the end of the game and I did not do it quick enough. That [game-tying] three that Dame [Lillard] made was on me. The first thing I did was apologize to our group for not being on point with that and getting that done quick enough."

On holding himself accountable for coaching mistakes even after victories:

"I would have done it if we lost, too, because I made a mistake. I just know if I can go in a locker room and say 'you didn't play hard' or 'you didn't do your job,' I've got to be held accountable, too. . . . It's just the right thing to do, to take ownership of what happened."

On scoring 30 points against Indiana after missing the previous game with an illness:

"I just wanted to be aggressive and try to put this team in a position to win."

On returning to action despite being sick lately:

On Mike Brown increasingly trusting his input and decision-making:

"Shoutout to the coach for trusting me more and more every day. And hearing me out. I'm appreciative of the trust this team has in me and the coaching staff has in me."

#2737. Why Sam Darnold starts for the Seahawks vs. 49ers in Week 12

The Seattle Seahawks face the San Francisco 49ers this weekend as the schedule gets tight, and the talk will center on Seattle's quarterback conundrum as it has all season. Sam Darnold is set to get the starting nod in Week 12 as he returns to health. He suffered a quad injury in Week 1 that held him out for Week 2, returned in Week 4, then appeared to re-aggravate it in Week 8 against the Chicago Bears. Meanwhile, backup quarterback Drew Lock has made the most of his opportunities when called upon. However, where the outside world sees controversy, the Seahawks see necessary depth.

Human beings are all completely different, no matter how you try to grade them on the same scale. The team has been hounded with questions about the recent "quarterback competition," to which they consistently respond that they're always evaluating, and they try to hint that Darnold is evaluated higher when both are healthy. Darnold's also the reigning Super Bowl champion and was one of the best quarterbacks throughout the entirety of the 2025 NFL playoffs. He was never a week away from losing the starting nod when healthy.

Last Thursday, I wrote about the scouting traits Darnold and Lock share, and why the Seahawks evaluated them highly enough to make moves for and trust them. I referenced former NFL scout and current draft analyst Daniel Jeremiah, who consistently refers back to his evaluation of Darnold's athleticism as "twitchier" than Lock's. That's why Darnold's able to battle the NFL's top quarterbacks like Matthew Stafford during the playoffs and even outplay them. Darnold will be the starter because he can play up to the level of elite teams like the 2025 Los Angeles Rams and 2026 49ers. It helped carry the Seahawks to Super Bowl LX, and it's the plan for attacking Super Bowl LXI.

Lock has filled in admirably while Darnold's been hurt, but there's a pecking order beyond what worked the week before. The team had a well-deserved Week 11 bye. Now they prepare for a massive opportunity against the NFC West leaders in Week 12. Head coach Mike Macdonald has said Darnold will start. He's the prototype. This team would feel as comfortable as any in the league putting their fate in the arms of two different quarterbacks this season. Neither is fully healthy, anyway — so the choice isn't all so simple, after all.

#2736. Amazon to acquire AI startup Decart's chip software, hire founders

Amazon is scooping up much of the technology and talent behind artificial intelligence startup Decart, marking the latest reverse acquihire deal by a major tech company.

In a Monday blog post, Amazon said it agreed to purchase Decart's "proprietary technology" and hire the startup's three founders, as well as their AI researchers and engineers.

The Israeli startup, founded in 2023, is best known for its software that lets developers sync their AI models across different chips, allowing them to switch hardware quickly, known as the Decart Optimization Stack.

"Every leap forward in AI starts with getting more out of every chip," Peter DeSantis, a senior vice president at Amazon who oversees foundation models, custom silicon and quantum computing development, said in a statement. "More efficient compute means better training, better reinforcement learning, faster inference, and better models for customers at a lower cost."

DeSantis added, "From our earliest work with Decart, we knew they were the kind of technologically fearless team who would help us push it even further."

Amazon has moved deeper into building custom AI chips amid insatiable demand for the technology, and as companies seek alternatives to Nvidia, the dominant provider. Amazon has positioned its Trainium and Inferentia chips as lower-cost options for AI developers.

Last month, Amazon CEO Andy Jassy said the company plans to make its Trainium chips externally available for the first time in "late in 2027."

Several outlets reported Amazon will pay $7.5 billion for Decart's assets, citing people familiar with the matter. Amazon declined to comment on the terms of the deal.

Anthropic had considered acquiring Decart and conducted due diligence, but ultimately walked away from a deal, Bloomberg reported.

Tech companies including Microsoft, Meta and Google have announced a series of deals with buzzy AI startups, which involve licensing their technology or nabbing their top talent and founders. The moves, often referred to as reverse acquihires, stop short of buying the companies outright. Amazon made two similar deals in 2024 when it brought on talent from Adept and Covariant.

The arrangements have attracted regulatory scrutiny in some cases. Amazon said Monday the Decart deal is subject to regulatory approval.

#2735. After Further Review: New York Jets-Los Angeles Chargers

After each game, we'll be sharing a comprehensive reivew that breaks down some of the controversial decisions from the officiating crew in the game. You may disagree in some cases, and that's fine…

Chargers 6-50 (leading to one Jets first down)Jets 4-18 (leading to two Chargers first downs)

Note: This does not include penalties that are declined or off-set.

Plays where the call was obvious, uncontroversial or not visible on broadcast footage

Olu Fashanu holding on a third down incompletion in the red zone. A very clear grab by Fashanu, who pulled back on the inside of Khalil Mack's shoulder pad as he went by him to prevent him from getting to Geno Smith.

Delay of game. Smith got frustrated here as he was unable to get everyone lined up in time and opted to just take the penalty rather than waste a timeout or rush to get the play off.

Jake Slaughter ineligible man downfield to negate a five-yard screen pass on 2nd-and-6. Clearly at least two yards downfield as the ball was being thrown, as his timing was a bit off. You only get a yard of leeway.

Delay of game on a punt. This didn't appear to be deliberate but they had plenty of time to get it off.

Jalyn Armour-Davis was flagged for going out of bounds as a gunner on a punt. He went out without being forced by a Jets player but did make the effort to get back in the field of play which is why it was five and not 15 yards.

Donte Jackson illegal contact to negate a Chargers sack. There was a slight grab on Adonai Mitchell as he made his route break, but this was a little soft.

Ladd McConkey offensive offside and Kayode Awosika offensive holding on a red zone incompletion. McConkey was definitely on the ball, so that was clear. The hold was arguably just as clear as Awosika had a handful of Darrell Jackson's jersey around the shoulder pad area and pulled his momentum away from pursuing the quarterback.

Cole Strange unsportsmanlike penalty after an extra point. The footage was not shown on the broadcast so it's unclear what he did, but it damaged the Chargers' late game strategy.

Roughing the passer on David Bailey to negate a third down red zone stop. This has already been discussed at length online. Of course it was very soft and not the kind of play the law is designed to prevent, and reminds us of many worse hits on Jets quarterbacks in recent history that went uncalled. Clearly Bailey didn't mean to hit Justin Herbert in the face mask as he was trying to deflect a pass, but his hand came down, made contact with Herbert's arm on his follow through and then hit Herbert's face mask. It wasn't deliberate, but by the letter of the law even though it was inadvertent, it was contact to the head/neck area and it was forcible because his hand was swiping downwards. He will probably end up still having to pay the fine on this one even if they appeal it. In isolation, this is probably hard to argue based on the rules as they are right now. This flag meant the Jets fell just 15 seconds short of a penalty-free first half.

Andrew Beck kickoff short of the landing zone to open the second half. This gave the Chargers the ball at their 40 and is probably one of the worst kickoffs you'll ever see, but that's part of the price you pay by having the extra tackler out there. He drilled it low, didn't seem to make clean contact and it bounced almost 15 yards short of the landing zone at the 34. This would have been even funnier if the Jets had been penalty-free at the half.

Azareye'h Thomas illegal hands to the face to negate a third down, red zone sack. This is the third time Thomas has been called for this in 2026, in exactly the same situation, and all of them have been soft. This one might actually have been the most justifiable of the three called, as he once again tried to jam with two hands to the chest in press coverage, but one hand slipped upwards into the side of the receiver's helmet. On the others, the receiver lowered his head to initiate the contact, but this wasn't the case here.

Kimani Vidal personal foul after a play. Vidal clearly threw a punch/shove at Malachi Moore's chest at the end of a scuffle. Herbert had been marked short of the marker on his 2nd-and-9 run, so this penalty would have made it 3rd-and-16. However, the spot was reviewed and the first down granted, which made it 1st-and-10 even though the ball was still moved back 15 yards.

Cam Hart defensive holding away from the ball on Sterling Shepard's first touchdown catch. Hart was covering Mitchell and there did appear to be a slight grab as Mitchell broke back towards the middle. Closer to the ball, Tony Jefferson grabbed Mason Taylor in almost identical fashion, but this was not flagged.

Logan Taylor ineligible man downfield on an incomplete pass which Herbert basically just shoveled out of bounds to avoid a loss of yardage. It was a surprise to see this called. Taylor had essentially stopped playing and was slowly jogging to a stop. Had Herbert not kind of double-clutched the toss out of bounds, he wouldn't even have been a yard downfield on the release and as it was it was only a yard and a half.

Here were some of the other notable missed calls, replay situations and controversial moments:

On the play mentioned above where Herbert was ruled to have picked up the first down after a review it was very close as to whether his foot came down inbounds or out of bounds, but this doesn't actually matter because the ball was level with the line to gain as it came down anyway, and then he crossed the sideline at that moment.

On Shepard's first touchdown he made a low diving catch and landed with the ball on about the half-yard line, but did have immediate control before then and the ball was across the plane of the goal line at the moment of control, so it was correctly ruled a touchdown.

McConkey had a catch ruled as an incompletion after Jamien Sherwood knocked the ball loose and it went out of bounds at the marker. The first official called it a catch and fumble but he was overruled by the second official. However, after a challenge, they reverted to the original call, which was correct as he made a football move in turning upfield and extending the ball.

On the fourth down play where D'Angelo Ponds bumped the running back towards the sideline, but he stayed inbounds and fell forwards for the first down, it probably didn't matter because when Ponds initially hit him, the ball was right at the marker, so he likely gets forward progress and a first down either way.

A flag was thrown and then picked up after a late on-field scuffle which wasn't shown on the broadcast footage. This stopped the clock which then meant there was a 10-second runoff, although that didn't particularly benefit or inconvenience either team on this occasion.

There was one ridiculous play where Joe Tippmann had his helmet ripped off by Teair Tart, but nothing was called.

On Omarion Hampton's longest run of the day, Tre Harris got away with a block in the back on Kingsley Enagbare.

Smith smartly threw the ball at Jeremy Ruckert's feet in the pocket to avoid a sack or intentional grounding call.

Taylor's rub route helped free Shepard up for his second touchdown as the defender hesitated. Contrast this with what happened last week when the defender just ran right into Taylor and got a flag from it.

Finally, Hall's knee was down with the ball short of the marker on second down when he picked up a late first down on the drive where the Jets ran out the clock. Any review would have been upstairs anyway because it was the last two minutes but the Chargers might actually have preferred a first down to 3rd-and-inches, because the latter could have given the Jets a chance to ice the game immediately.

Let us know what we missed – or misinterpreted – in the comments…

#2733. SpaceX sued by Oregon and Virginia over AI deepfake porn, Grok safety claims

Lawsuits against Elon Musk's SpaceX are stacking up, with Virginia and Oregon becoming the first states to sue the company over issues with its Grok AI image generator.

Versions of Grok and SpaceX's social network X have enabled users to generate and distribute explicit deepfakes based on real photos or videos of children and other people who did not consent, according to research by the Internet Watch Foundation, the Center for Countering Digital Hate and others.

In separate complaints submitted to state courts as of Monday morning, Oregon Attorney General Dan Rayfield and Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones argued that SpaceX failed to put safeguards in place to stop Grok and X users from making and sharing harmful deepfake images, especially alleged AI-generated child sexual abuse material.

The complaints also allege that SpaceX has falsely represented its AI apps and social network (Grok and X) as generally safe, which the states see as a violation of Virginia's Consumer Protection Act or Oregon's Unlawful Trade Practices Act. The matter is particularly concerning because X allows minors 13 and older to access its content.

Rayfield and Jones both noted in their complaints that harms caused by non-consensual, sexualized images go beyond the victims depicted in the images.

In a statement emailed to CNBC by a spokesperson for SpaceXSI, and posted on the company's @Safety account on X, the company said, "When someone tries to break our safeguards and generate this content, we take appropriate measures, including actioning accounts and notifying law enforcement. Attempting to create this material is a serious crime, and the individuals who do it should be prosecuted to the fullest extent of the law. We will continue to work with the authorities via all available means to pursue these bad actors."

At an NYC Council hearing in October, a SpaceX AI policy leader, Gabriela Castillo Madrid, said the company had appropriate "safeguards" and "strict controls" in place around its AI products.

SpaceX was already facing multiple Grok- and X-related lawsuits, and government probes around the world, including by authorities in the U.K., Spain, Ireland, France and the state of California.

In private-sector lawsuits filed against SpaceX in the U.S. concerning Grok and AI-generated, explicit images, litigation is still in early stages and is tracked by The Center for Internet Technology Addiction.

When the city of Baltimore sued xAI, now owned and operated by SpaceX, the company argued that under Section 230, they were not liable for unwanted images. Section 230 is a federal law that largely shields online platforms from liability for users' content.

SpaceX also sued a user in a federal court in the Northern District of Texas for breach of contract after he allegedly used Grok to create and distribute deepfake explicit images, including depictions of minors. Lawyers for Musk's company argued that Grok should be seen as "a neutral tool, subject to user control," and that any image generation "is the result of the user's prompts and directions."

The new state suits add to legal pressure on SpaceX. Both Virginia and Oregon are seeking injunctions, civil penalties, disgorgement and legal costs, while Virginia is also seeking restitution for consumers from Musk's company.

Oregon submitted its complaint to the Multnomah County Circuit Court on Sunday evening, and Virginia submitted its complaint to the City of Richmond Circuit Court Monday morning. As of early Monday, the suits were pending the courts' acceptance.

State attorneys general have recently succeeded in compelling U.S. social media companies to change their products and pay for alleged past harms.

In August, for example, Meta settled a major federal case centered on allegations that the social media giant misrepresented the level of child mental health problems caused by its apps, Facebook and Instagram. Without admitting to wrongdoing, Meta agreed to pay states around $17 billion over a 10-year period and to make app changes that experts believe will help keep children safer online.

#2730. Toronto faces struggling Bucks

The Toronto Raptors kick off a three-game homestand tonight, facing off against the Milwaukee Bucks in their second matchup of the season. In their last contest, Toronto won handily, 121-107 despite missing Immanuel Quickley, Scottie Barnes, and Kawhi Leonard. That shouldn't be surprising though, as Milwaukee has come out of the gate slowly this season, holding a brutal 1-16 record.

Despite it being close in the first quarter of that game, Toronto was eventually able to run away with it due, in big part, to the contributions of Jamal Shead and Alijah Martin. Shead started the game and set a career high 16 assists. His facilitation was integral throughout, boasting a +23 point differential in his minutes on the floor. Alijah Martin's contribution was scoring, going 6-7 from long range to finish with 27 points. He had the second highest point differential, +22 from the floor. Without him, the team was a combined 3-25 from long range, one of their less than savoury shooting performances of the season. Although they struggled shooting, their rebounding, ball movement, and defence allowed them to stay ahead.

Milwaukee was carried by rookie Brayden Burries and Tyler Herro, who both had 20-point performances. It wasn't enough though, especially as they shot a brutal 10-40 from the field. That said it's only marginally worse than their previous performances this season, averaging a brutal 34% from long range as a team. This is a rebuilding year for them, especially after the loss of Giannis in the offseason and they haven't really found their identity yet.

To be successful again, they should focus on their defence and ball movement. Milwaukee commits a lot of turnovers, usually about 16 per game. Last time, Toronto managed to get them to turn the ball over 20 times, along with generating 12 steals and 4 blocks. Toronto continues to be in the top-half of the league defensively and despite some late game execution, they've made it tough for teams to score, especially given some excellent individual performances from Barnes, CMB, and Shead to name a few. They also play better when they're not forcing isolation possessions, looking for the open man, and finding open shots. Sometimes they panic, and usually the result is an empty possession. Toronto is also young and learning though, so the more they can work on their offensive system and ball movement the better.

They've gotten past their biggest hurdle in beating a winning team on Friday against the Pistons. That game also provided an opportunity for rookie Allen Graves to have a breakout 20 point, 10 rebound performance that should earn him another starting spot tonight.

Being at home and playing a couple weaker teams will hopefully give Toronto the opportunity to develop some momentum and work their way back towards 0.500. And what better chance to start then facing the Bucks again?

#2729. Buccaneers grades: Costly mistakes define Week 11 loss to Lions

The Tampa Bay Buccaneers fell to 3-7 following Sunday's 31-21 loss to the Detroit Lions, and the final score doesn't fully capture how frustrating the afternoon was. Tampa Bay had opportunities to take control of the game but repeatedly undermined itself with penalties, poor execution, and communication problems. The Buccaneers fought back to within three points late in the third quarter before Detroit scored 14 unanswered points in the fourth.

Individual performances were worth recognizing, particularly from rookie receiver Ted Hurst III and running back Bucky Irving. However, the Buccaneers struggled to put together a complete performance against a Detroit team that capitalized on their mistakes.

Here is how each position group graded out following the loss.

Baker Mayfield finished 20-of-33 passing for 187 yards and two touchdowns without throwing an interception. He also rushed for 36 yards on six attempts, showing his willingness to extend plays despite taking significant punishment throughout the afternoon. Mayfield was sacked four times and fumbled on Tampa Bay's opening possession, although the Buccaneers recovered.

Mayfield deserves credit for his toughness and several impressive throws, including the 23-yard touchdown to Ted Hurst before halftime. However, he occasionally created additional pressure by leaving the pocket early, and his second touchdown came with the outcome essentially decided. His individual effort was encouraging, but the passing offense lacked consistency when Tampa Bay needed it most.

Bucky Irving finished with 68 rushing yards and a touchdown on 18 carries while adding three receptions for 32 yards. His 3.8 yards per carry doesn't fully reflect his performance, especially given the contact he absorbed and how hard Tampa Bay had to create running lanes.

Irving demonstrated excellent balance, patience, and agility while repeatedly fighting through defenders for additional yardage. Luke Goedeke's holding penalty erased his apparent 80-yard touchdown run, eliminating what could have been the offense's biggest play. Kenny Gainwell also contributed 16 rushing yards and four receptions for 43 yards. The production wasn't overwhelming, but the running backs made the most of several difficult situations.

Ted Hurst provided the biggest positive for Tampa Bay's receiving corps, catching five passes for 56 yards and two touchdowns. His 23-yard scoring reception before halftime was one of the Buccaneers' best offensive plays, and his second touchdown capped an encouraging individual performance.

The rest of the group struggled to make an impact. Chris Godwin finished with three receptions for 13 yards, Emeka Egbuka caught two passes for 18 yards on five targets, and Tez Johnson added two receptions for 17 yards. Egbuka's limited production was particularly disappointing considering his importance to the offense. Hurst deserves recognition, but the group collectively needed to produce more.

Cade Otton caught one pass for eight yards on three targets, while Brandon Sharp failed to record a reception on his lone target. Tampa Bay received little production from the position through the passing game, limiting Mayfield's options when Detroit applied pressure.

Otton contributed as a blocker, particularly on Tampa Bay's screen passes, but the tight ends were largely absent as receiving threats. The Buccaneers need more consistent involvement from the position, especially when their offense is struggling to move the ball through conventional passing concepts.

Tampa Bay allowed four sacks for 33 yards and struggled to protect against Detroit's defensive front. The offensive line also failed to create enough movement on consecutive short-yardage attempts during the first quarter, resulting in a turnover on downs.

The most damaging mistake came when Goedeke's holding penalty erased Irving's apparent 80-yard touchdown run. Mayfield occasionally contributed to the pressure by extending plays or leaving the pocket early, so the offensive line shouldn't absorb all the blame. However, the protection breakdowns and inability to consistently win at the line of scrimmage made this one of Tampa Bay's weakest units.

Tampa Bay's defensive front produced some encouraging moments, particularly from David Walker and Vita Vea. Walker recorded the Buccaneers' only sack, bringing Jared Goff down for a two-yard loss early in the fourth quarter. He also contributed two tackles for loss and two quarterback hits despite playing just 19 defensive snaps.

Vea disrupted Detroit's opening possession with strong run support and a pass breakup, but the Buccaneers struggled to generate consistent pressure all afternoon. Goff finished with 279 passing yards and two touchdowns while taking just one sack. Detroit also found success running the football when it needed to close out the game, leaving the defensive front with a mixed performance.

Jeremiah Trotter Jr. led Tampa Bay with 14 combined tackles, while Alex Anzalone added seven. The linebackers were involved throughout the afternoon, but Detroit repeatedly found openings across the middle of Tampa Bay's zone coverage. The coverage difficulties weren't limited to the linebackers, although the unit was part of a defense that struggled to consistently close passing lanes.

Detroit's tight ends were particularly effective, with Sam LaPorta, Brock Wright and Tyler Conklin combining for six receptions and 122 yards. Trotter also committed a costly 15-yard facemask penalty during Detroit's fourth-quarter touchdown drive. Tampa Bay made some important stops, but the linebackers and surrounding coverage needed to do more to disrupt Goff's passing options.

Tampa Bay entered the game without Antoine Winfield Jr. and quickly encountered additional problems when Jacob Parrish suffered an injury during Detroit's first touchdown drive. Benjamin Morrison took on a larger role before also being ruled out later in the game, forcing another personnel adjustment. Kevin Knowles II subsequently contributed at outside cornerback, playing 20 defensive snaps.

The injuries created a difficult situation, but Detroit capitalized on opportunities downfield. Jameson Williams finished with four receptions for 93 yards, including a 57-yard touchdown, while Amon-Ra St. Brown added another score. Morrison appeared involved in coverage on both touchdowns, although the exact defensive responsibilities remain unclear. His later absence was officially reported as an injury-related game status, so it would be unfair to suggest he was benched for his performance. The circumstances deserve consideration, but the secondary still surrendered too many important completions.

Chase McLaughlin converted all three extra-point attempts, while Riley Dixon averaged 49.3 yards on four punts, placing two inside Detroit's 20-yard line. Walker also delivered an excellent coverage tackle early in the game, providing one of Tampa Bay's better special teams moments.

However, Walker was also flagged for a false start on the punt unit during the second quarter, costing Tampa Bay five yards before Dixon's next attempt. The Buccaneers failed to generate a meaningful return that could change the game's momentum. There were solid individual contributions, particularly from McLaughlin and Dixon, but the unit's overall performance was fairly ordinary.

The Buccaneers finished with eight penalties for 58 yards, including two delay-of-game penalties, and repeatedly struggled to get organized before the snap. Ford Field's crowd was loud all afternoon, and Tampa Bay appeared to struggle in that environment. Whether the problems stemmed from late play calls, poor communication in the huddle, or misaligned personnel, the offense consistently found itself rushing against the play clock.

Todd Bowles' decision to go for it on fourth-and-1 early in the game deserves scrutiny. Being aggressive against a talented Detroit team has value, and Bowles was clearly trying to establish an approach that could put pressure on the Lions. However, with the game still scoreless and Chase McLaughlin available, taking the field goal would have been reasonable. Tampa Bay instead turned the ball over on downs after Irving was stopped behind the line of scrimmage.

The more concerning sequence came late in the fourth quarter. Facing fourth-and-14, the Buccaneers hurried the snap rather than using a timeout to organize the offense. Mayfield was sacked for a nine-yard loss, giving Detroit possession at Tampa Bay's 34-yard line. The Lions eventually scored another touchdown, extending their lead to 31-14. Whether a timeout would have changed the result is impossible to know, but the repeated pre-snap confusion made the decision to rush into such an important play difficult to defend.

Tampa Bay showed enough individual talent to stay competitive, but recurring mistakes and communication problems were hard to overlook. The Buccaneers have seven games remaining,

#2728. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2726. Super Bowl LXI is heading to IMAX cinemas. How to get tickets

Super Bowl is coming to IMAX cinemas near you for the first time in history.

The National Football League (NFL), ESPN, Walt Disney Company and IMAX are partnering to bring the Super Bowl to IMAX theaters for the first time in ESPN's 47-year history, "giving fans a new, larger-than-life way to experience America's biggest sporting event."

ESPN's telecast of Super Bowl LXI will be shown at hundreds of IMAX locations across the U.S. and in select international markets, including Australia, New Zealand and Brazil, a Disney news release said, adding the screening will mark "a milestone in cinema" by being the "largest live event in IMAX history."

This year's broadcast will be ABC's first Super Bowl telecast since 2006, when the network aired Super Bowl 40.

"The Super Bowl is a one-of-a-kind global cultural event imbued with spectacle, sound, drama, and heroes — all the makings of a great IMAX experience," IMAX CEO Rich Gelfond said in a statement. "This is a milestone for IMAX that further elevates our standing as a premier global platform for entertainment and events, and we're excited to work in partnership with Disney, ESPN and the NFL to give football fans an unforgettable night."

ESPN's Monday Night Football broadcast team of Joe Buck, Troy Aikman, Lisa Salters and Laura Rutledge will call the game.

Super Bowl LXI is scheduled for Sunday, Feb. 14, 2027, Valentine's Day, with kickoff at 6 p.m. ET. The big game will be at SoFi Stadium in Los Angeles for a second time.

Disney said the event's expansion into IMAX "extends the reach of the Super Bowl across more platforms than ever," and creates "an entirely new opportunity for fans to come together and experience the NFL's premier game in a communal setting."

IMAX theater tickets for Super Bowl LXI are expected to go on sale in January 2027, according to the news release. A date has not yet been specified.

Disney said additional details, including a list of participating theater locations and ticketing information, will be released in the coming weeks. For more information, fans can visit IMAX.com or local theaters that sell IMAX tickets.

#2725. Jorge Masvidal: 'Total package' Kevin Vallejos should get next UFC title shot

Kevin Vallejos has a strong supporter in Jorge Masvidal.

Although it was a highly competitive fight with somewhat arguable scorecards, the 24-year-old left with the victory.

Former UFC BMF champion and "Deep Waters" panelist Masvidal thought Vallejos was going into be in for a tough night at the office against Sterling. He got through, and that shows Masvidal that he's ready to challenge 145-pound champ Alexander Volkanovski (29-4 MMA, 16-3 UFC) for the gold.

"I thought it was maybe the worst stylistic matchup they could give to Vallejos, and close or not, he won," Masvidal said on Monday's episode of "Deep Waters" on Paramount+. "We've really got to consider this guy up there. To me, stylistically, the worst matchup for him was Sterling by far.

"I'd like to see him fight for the title, to tell you the truth. Right now: Fight Volk."

Following his UFC 333 title defense over Movsar Evloev in October, Volkanovski pitched the idea of moving to lightweight for a champ-vs-champ showdown with Justin Gaethje. If that doesn't materialize, Volkanovski said he would look at the next contender in his own weight class.

Since then, it's narrowed down to a two-man race between Jean Silva and now Vallejos. Masvidal sees them both as worthy, but he thinks Vallejos has superior tools to deal with what Volkanovski brings to the table.

"Even when Aljo got in, he had some really good scrambles," Masvidal said. "I think he's the total package at 145."

#2724. Tech stocks see monster rally after Jensen Huang returns as Nvidia CEO

Major tech stocks rebounded across the board Monday, sharply reversing Friday's steep sell-off when artificial intelligence stocks fell in the Nasdaq's worst week since 2020.

Shares of Nvidia surged 31.48% after the company announced Monday morning that Jensen Huang was reinstated as CEO. The semiconductor giant gained back nearly $1 trillion in market cap, posting its best day since May 2003.

Nvidia's surge and Huang's reinstatement followed a more than 40% slump in the stock over the past month. The company has been embattled as reports surfaced about Huang's multiple extramarital affairs — one of which was with a woman U.S. intelligence reportedly believes to be a Chinese spy. Huang resigned as CEO of the company he founded on Nov. 12.

The Nasdaq Composite surged 6.51% Monday, for its best day since April 9, 2025. The Nasdaq slid 10% last week, with tech stocks having a rocky week as investors remained nervous about the valuation of some AI companies and business models. All three major averages ended last week in correction territory, and the Nasdaq turned negative for 2026.

Shares of Big Tech companies and other stocks related to the AI trade saw a lift Monday on the Nvidia news.

Shares of Alphabet surged 6.34%, Meta gained 6.55%, Amazon jumped 3.47% and Microsoft increased 1.65%. SpaceX added 4.35%, Arm Holdings surged 16.08% and AMD added 6.85%. Dell and Micron posted some of the sector's biggest gains of the day, gaining 20.46% and 19.04%, respectively. The iShares Semiconductor ETF gained 13.63%.

Neoclouds, which have seen a particularly choppy few months amid AI boom financing concerns, surged on Huang's reinstatement. Nebius soared 20.28%, CoreWeave gained 22.32% and Iren gained 23.86%.

Following a brief premarket surge, Anthropic shares posted a decline of 1.43% in its third day of trading. Anthropic raised a record $93.5 billion in its IPO. The stock gained 17% in its debut on Thursday, before closing down 8.5% on Friday.

Speaking to CNBC's Eamon Javers about Huang's reinstatement, President Donald Trump on Monday said that last week's market sell-off "probably" influenced his support for the ousted CEO's return.

"Probably so, but the bigger impact was the long term. The bigger impact was, he's got other chips in his brain that nobody else knows about," Trump said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street."

Trump said he spoke to Nvidia's board directly and that he "convinced" the board to reinstate Huang, who the president said "did nothing wrong."

"That thing's like a rocket ship going in the wrong direction. Now it's a rocket ship going in the right direction, and it's taking the whole market with it," Trump said, referring to Nvidia's stock price.

"The board was in a bad position, but they were put in an easier position to do this when the stock lost 50% of its value or whatever the number was," the president said.

#2723. Improvement in this key area taken Wembanyama to the next level

No one can stop Victor Wembanyama when he aims for the heart. The whole league should be on notice because he has more lobs than each team and another leap is happening in real time. A good chunk of it has to do with his screening.

Former NBA player and Harvard man, Jeremy Lin, highlighted Wembanyama's screening on his Instagram page, which all San Antonio Spurs fans should check out. He touched on four distinct screens — the 45° angle, flat-ball screen in transition, traditional and the 45° angle with his back facing the backcourt line.

Setting picks obviously gets teammates open, but doing it correctly and quickly enough also gets the screener in great position to make a play. Wembanyama's wide base is a significant advantage in this area and so is his catch radius as it is often out of reach of defenders.

The 45° angle is nice because it starts at the blind side and it blocks the defender's inside leg from moving cleanly; the flat-ball screen in transition gets helped by a roll and that movement helps set the space for the pick; Wemby's traditional screen is more effective than most due to his size; and the hardest of these to guard is the 45° angle with his back facing mid-court. As Lin explained, this is done close to the baseline, limiting the on-ball defenders ability to go underneath. Yet, in fairness to the defenders, what are they supposed to do when they run up against a mountain of an offensive lineman? This play is a cheat code that can only be neutralized at the expense of the 3-point line — and he might still go over the top.

This season, Wembanyama has upped his percentage of shots at the rim to 36 percent from 32.8 percent last year. He converts 80.3 percent of the shots he takes there and is on pace to record the most dunks of his career. Stephon Castle has been the playmaker with the best connection with him this season, averaging the most passes and the highest percentage leading to shots from those passes.

Castle also deserves some recognition in the dunking area, because of all the registered dunks in the NBA this season, the five highest vertical leaps recorded were his, between 38.3 and 41.5 inches. Wemby, on the other hand, does not need to get off the ground that much and has even recorded one this year, lifting off 11.6 inches from the ground.

Furthermore, Wemby deserves props because on top of being ultra efficient, he has done it in an exciting way. Alley-oops are arguably the most entertaining plays in basketball, so it's a privilege to see a team and specifically one player finish them in high volume. One would probably need to ask the NBA's communications office for help tracking which player has finished the most lobs to himself, but I'm sure it's him, too. He's done some of those as he improvised, making them more special.

Screens, like rebounds, are winning plays that make life for everyone easier. Now that Wembanyama has gotten great at using them for himself, the next area is using more of those to free up his teammates with some off-ball screens. He's lower than he should be in screen-assists created (1.8) and can easily pick this area up if he's told to. It would help create mismatches which could lead to more lobs.

He's the league leader in points in the paint (270 total), and he has done all of this without sacrificing his style of being an outside player, as he is averaging the most 3-point attempts of his career (6.5). He's not quite doing it all yet, since his efficiency is low from behind ghe arc, but he is close to getting there.

#2720. DNI Jay Clayton tapped as acting national cyber director

Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton has been tapped to serve as acting national cyber director while the Trump administration's permanent nominee for the role awaits Senate confirmation, a White House official told CNBC on Monday.

Trump had already added artificial intelligence czar to Clayton's portfolio in October. The official asked not to be named because the pick hasn't been announced.

The White House last week announced that President Donald Trump had picked Kirsten Davies, the Pentagon's chief information officer, to lead the Office of the National Cyber Director.

The U.S. cyber director advises the president on cybersecurity and coordinates U.S. cyber policy across federal agencies. Federal law states that a person may not serve as an acting officer if the president has submitted that person's nomination to the Senate for full appointment and if that person served as first assistant to the office for less than 90 days.

Davies would replace Sean Cairncross, who was confirmed to the role in August 2025 and whose departure was first reported by Axios last week.

Trump, at a White House press conference on Friday, said he decided to replace Cairncross "because I didn't think that person was good enough."
